ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e84	On 18 June 2009, the previous Grandfather’s presidency appointed a selection to propose a limited revision of the Constitution with the aim of strengthening the position of human rights. Former Wage Representative Inge Wage became the leader of the sample. Let me stress that although it is mentioned in the mandate of the sample that this is due to the 200th anniversary of the Constitution in 2014, it is very clear in the mandate that it is to strengthen the position of human rights in the Constitution that is the decisive basis for the appointment of the sample. It is important to stress because part of the professional legal environment has pointed out that celebrating the 200th anniversary of the Constitution itself is not a good reason to change the Constitution. I agree. It's not a celebration, but it's a confession that a lot is missing in the Constitution. But this isn't a part of the celebration. The new build-up is free for the next three years to adopt what is available from different variants. It was a broad composite sample. All batches have proposed representatives in the sample and the sample submitted its report in January 2012. Then the report was published, of course. In Document No: The proposals were presented for 2011–2012 and the report was forwarded by the Presidency to the Monitoring and Constitutional Committee for Treatment. This time, not to give up, of course, because you cannot vote on Constitutional proposals, it must be a constitutional proposal, signed by representatives, but in order to deal with the report as broad as possible by inviting you to a broad consultation. It has been the main purpose of the work of the Committee. A broad consultation has been carried out, as explained in the vote. Let me say it so that Constitutional proposals have never had a broad popular appeal. If you look back in time at the times when the Constitution has been revised, and this applies to approx. Two-thirds of today's constitutions, there has rarely been any requirement for people to do so, but it is actually one of the main tasks of Parliament, the Stortinget, to carry out regular audits. Sometimes constitutional provisions have aroused political debate, not least in our vote on membership of the EEC and the EU, the European Union, but it has been more the case and not the Constitution that has given rise to debate. That was the background for us to invite you to the widest possible consultation. And let me say it like this, the whole legal environment, in both Oslo and Bergen and the rest of the country, engaged quite strongly in written inquiries, and seminars were organized in which the members of the committee attended. It was a debate over several months. Against this background, constitutional proposals were put forward by members of the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee, and I stress members, with different options. In this way, it has been sought to facilitate the possibility of the next enlargement being as free as possible to deal with the existing proposals. Then I want to add that it means enough that there is a proposal here that is not necessarily personal and behind it, but that is intended to give the next big one, which has decision-making power in relation to these proposals, an opportunity to look at different alternatives – and there are different alternatives. I don't think it's one of the professionally qualified recordings we've received, which are not taken into account in this context by adding their alternatives to the proposals promoted by the Wage Committee. I remember that the Wage Committee worked for almost two years based on significant political and professional legal insight. Rarely have we seen the preparation of constitutional proposals that have been more extensive and thorough professional and political than this process. I think we should send a thank you to the previous presidency, to the former prime minister, Mr Jagland, for the initiative for this thorough treatment. For we must be honest, and we must do so from this platform, it is not always that constitutional proposals are the proposals that are being prepared as far and as far as possible, even if they are important enough. This process has been different. It has distinguished itself by seeking the widest possible proposal. In fact, there is a setting from the Wage Selection with an unusually few dissertations. In my opinion, the Constitution is a valuable document, which is designed to reflect both fundamental values in a society and the rights of a society. If you read today's constitution, it is, as I said earlier, approx. two thirds of the provisions changed, so that the Constitution has been subject to a gradual change over many years. A large period of time without changes to the Constitution is one of the exceptions, i.e. without consideration of draft constitutions being adopted. Today's society is different than it was in 1814, and it's different than it was in 1948. Why do I choose 1948? Yes, it was the year of the adoption of the UN Universal Declaration of Human Rights. There have also been quite significant changes since then. I would like to quote from the Committee's position, page 7 - and there is no disagreement about this: ‘The main societal changes are the economic and technological development, the emergence of the welfare society, the educational explosion, the agreement that there should be equal opportunities for women and men, an increased understanding of children’s rights, and an increase in the number of Nordics with different ethnic, linguistic, religious and cultural backgrounds. This multicultural and modern welfare country should therefore be reflected in the Fundamental Law’s rights provisions, without overshadowing the fundamental civil and political rights of the Constitution since 1814.’» I think that in many ways also answers the question: Is pure welfare rights right to be placed in the Constitution? To that I would say that this is nothing new. Today's constitution also contains such fundamental provisions on the right to work, the right to work, the right to a healthy and good environment, etc. I remember that the rights must also be designed in principle. So there's nothing in rights about, for example, the right to education, the right to health, the right to a satisfactory standard of living and social protection which state something about the political decisions on which rights, their scope, the rates to be applied, etc. But it gives a framework, and it gives an interpretation. In this there is also a danger that some have seen, in the so-called judicial process. Do we need basic laws – or, more generally, should we have a society that ensures that individual rights govern policy, or should policy govern rights? That's an interesting and fundamental question. I don't think the Constitution is what really creates the distinction in terms of the legal process in a society. I see the danger that excessive legalisation can make policy less important. There are moves, for example: in the American society that I think is reminiscent of this – unlike our own society. But legalisation in this regard, I think, is a derailment of the debate. That's why I finally want to quote from a chronicle that formerly British President Thorbjørn Jagland wrote in the Evening Post on March 7th this year, and I agree with these views on legalization. He's actually quoting a statement from Mr. Hallgeir H. Langeland, and it's a great honour, I'd say, to be quoted with a view to weakening the rule of the people. Jagland wrote the following: ‘It is well known that government and state apparatus regard this as a danger. But it surprises me that the Stortinget does not perceive its role differently. Democracy must have counter-powers to control and balance a often bureaucratic state apparatus. The role of size is to establish such counter-power. We have seen it so often: the courts are not a threat to citizens' rights, but it can be a faceless bureaucracy.' I basically share these views and believe that these thoughts should also follow the case further. I do not want to go into individual provisions that have been controversial, because the final decisions will be taken later, but I have put forward a few fundamental views on what a constitution should be like. And I think that the proposals that are available also represent the diversity of views that are in the matter and that give the next big deal – in its first three years – a good opportunity to carry out a proper and timely revision of the Constitution so that general human rights are reflected in the Universal Declaration – economic and social rights – and the European Convention on Human Rights are also reflected in the Norwegian Constitution.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e124	I start by saying something that is, of course, that the Constitution is owned by the Stortinget, and that it is the elected representatives who are proposers of constitutional changes. It is therefore not the parties as such who support the proposals that we are debating here today. But it is just as full as the rapporteur, Foss, has just said, that the place of human rights in the Constitution has been given a very special treatment that goes far beyond what is common before constitutional proposals are promoted by individual representatives. As the representative Foss also said, it was then Prime Minister Jagland who took the initiative in 2009 to strengthen the human rights of the Constitution and which led the Presidency of the United Kingdom on 18 June 2009 to set up a selection to investigate and promote proposals for a limited revision of the Constitution with the aim of strengthening the position of human rights in national law by giving the status of fundamental human rights. The committee's work was intended to form part of Storting's preparations for the foundation's 200th anniversary in 2014. The committee that was led by Inge Wohnning, which I see is also present here today, gave its report to the Presidentship of the Stortinge on December 19, 2011. The Presidency then forwarded the report to the Control and Constitutional Committee for further consideration. We in the Labour Party have carried out a thorough examination, and the Workers' Party's representatives in the Control and Constitution Committee are behind the overall assessment of the Human Rights Committee – with the exception that we will come back to in the debate on the part of the Workers' Party. I would like first of all to give some consideration to how the Workers' Party – although I would like to emphasise again that there are individual representatives who are proposers – and how we, as representatives of the Control and Constitutional Committee, have assessed the report of the Human Rights Committee. But first of all, I would like to commend the presidency for taking the initiative of this debate on the constitutional proposals that have been promoted in this period and which are to be dealt with by the next majority. It is in the spirit of the Constitution and, hopefully, will give the voters insight and opportunity to consider the constitutional proposals that have been put forward before they go to the elections in September. Before I go into the assessment of human rights in the Constitution, I think it is in its place to briefly repeat some of the premises on which the Human Rights Committee has based its work. Firstly, the Constitution does not give a complete picture of the human rights protection that actually exists in Norwegian law, as there are only some of the central human rights that have been expressed in the Constitution. Today’s fragmentation could lead to legal uncertainty as to whether the values expressed are more important or more overarching than human rights in other legislation. Major public investigations into discrimination and privacy have concluded that the protection of fundamental rights must be strengthened. Secondly, the protection of fundamental rights should at least cover the key human rights underlying the international human rights conventions to which Norway has acceded. Thirdly, the fundamental legislating of the central human rights will not lead to what is often referred to as the legalisation of society. Fourth, the selection has taken into account how human rights are expressed in modern institutions in Western Europe, especially in our neighbouring countries. Attention has been paid to Norwegian traditions and Norwegian values in the sense of making the values of the Norwegian society more visible and in view of the fact that the Constitution should be better suited to meeting Norway’s future challenges. Finally, the human rights of the Constitution are brought together in a new section E dealing only with human rights. In the proceedings of the Workers' Party, we have, of course, been aware that these prerequisites have also been and are subject to debate and different perceptions both within and outside the legal environment, and that there have been differing views on both the outcome of the work of the Human Rights Committee and the process both before and after the reduction of the sample. Our starting point is that it is important to have a relatively conservative starting point in terms of changes to the Constitution. The basic law should remain general and not detailed – this in order for it to remain as it has been, since it was written almost 200 years ago. On the other hand, we must not become so constitutional conservative that we do not provide for a constitution that can capture and reflect the new reality of society, and that does not look ahead sufficiently. Changes now proposed may seem natural and unnecessary today, but they may not be in 50 years' time. The aim of constitutional changes must be to guarantee our grandchildren and grandchildren the same fundamental rights that we enjoy in Norwegian society today. This is the overall perspective of the changes that the Workers' Party has made. We have therefore taken from the present amendments of the Human Rights Committee which they presented in their report. Many of them are, in our view, unproblematic and can be seen as a foundation for the model of society we have today, and the Workers' Party believes to have much of the honor and pride of. In our view, it's important in such a perspective to actually base this model. I think the proposal that exists balances well the various considerations that need to be taken into account. Quote from selection: ‘One basic idea must be that the Constitution should preserve key values in today’s Norwegian society, while reflecting the values that have survived over time. Benchmarking these values will be a contribution to ensuring that future generations will also benefit from these rights.’ I also agree with the Human Rights Committee that there is a need to update the Constitution in relation to the society we live today. This does not mean that the Constitution should provide us with the answers to difficult and important societal challenges, but that it should crush some key principles and fundamental values that currently form the basis for the decisions taken. In this way, to a greater extent, it will be possible to ensure that the individual's freedom, equality and human dignity are safeguarded in decision-making processes in our future as well. For the Workers' Party, some of the proposals are problematic and may run counter to important principles that our party has fought, such as: abortion laws. We have therefore made our own alternative formulations in which our views are respected and put in place as our own constitutional proposals. This will come back to the other representatives of the Workers' Party in the Control and Constitutional Committee and will explain later in the debate. It is the next big deal to deal with the proposals we are debating here today, as the representative Foss has also stressed. It has been very important for us in the Workers' Party to create a large room for action for those who are going to do the important work that is to change the Constitution for the next growing-up period.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e228	The proposal is to strengthen the position of human rights in the Constitution. As others have pointed out, I would like to stress the need for the debate and commend those who have helped to secure it, a debate that gives greater attention to how to safeguard human rights and how to follow up human rights. Here, there's legitimate doubt in many environments about what's wisest. My soul is among these doubts. What Langeland’s representative had to say about housing is an example of how wise it is to include such questions in the Constitution. The presidency of the Grand Duchy appointed a committee chaired by Inge Wohnung, and the mandate was to propose limited revision – and I would like to underline it first: limited revision – with the aim of strengthening the position of human rights in national law. It is therefore a significant space for action, a limited revision and strengthening the position of human rights. We are faced with a demanding political judgement as to how we are to implement this. Thorbjørn Jagland was the President of Stortinget in the previous period and one of the initiators of the reduction of the selection of which Inge Wohnning was the leader. The current Secretary-General of the Council of Europe, Thorbjørn Jagland, said in the evening post on 7 March this year that we have 'ended with a foundation law that is stiff and immature'. I strongly disagree with such a reference to the Constitution. The Constitution has been and is invaluable for Norway. It has given a firm anchoring in times of crisis, and it has been guiding under changing social conditions. Its overall authority and its great wear and tear are both due to the fact that it has had its basis in basic values with which the people have identified themselves and that it has been renewed through the change and addition of paragraphs as well as through custom. It is precisely a characteristic of our constitution that it combines stability and renewal. It is also characteristic that the renewal has taken place in the development of democracy and as a result of reasons which the development of our society has made reasonable. According to Thorbjørn Jagland’s opinion, ‘the struggle for ‘Norwegian’ should be linked to the heaviest trends of time in the international community – which are undoubtedly human rights’. Human rights and the question of which of them should be included in the Constitution and how best to realise them in Europe are important. However, in terms of current trends, the dominant trend in Europe now seems to be that non-elected – directors, secretaries-general, commissioners and other international leaders – tell elected representatives what is important. The most overriding value of the Constitution lies in ensuring democracy. Of this value, a number of other values and rights can be derived. I believe that we must have a much more thorough debate before taking a stand on what human rights should possibly be included in the Constitution. For me, it's important that it's not just ideal goals, but rights that we believe need to be realised, and that we can realize, that's central. So that's why what we have to and can realize is important to me in the constitutional changes, not just idealistic desires. The Constitution must ensure that power is achieved and exercised through democratic, political processes. I have more faith in democracy than in the jessen, in the sense that democracy must be supreme in order to ensure justice and fairness in our society. The more right-wing logic that prevails in the governance of society, the more it can lead to a legalisation and a transfer of power from elected assemblies to the courts. Lawyers have played an important role in the development of our democratic rule, both as a profession and as actors in political life. The efforts of many university lawyers have been particularly important, and in the further development of our society, lawyers will have an important role, but they must not have greater power and influence. In particular, we should be reluctant to surrender more power to the lawyers of the international and supranational bodies. The power should lie in the elected assemblies where the representatives are not only elected but also accountable to the people who have elected them. Political change and democracy are crucial. Much can be done better in our country. The best way to do that is to secure democracy through an informative and sharp change of words. The example of that is that in connection with the accession to the European Union, because democracy is the only form of government in which those who are governed have power over those who rule. I chose here to make a basic post without going into the content of the individual proposals. It is the role of the elected representatives that is central, and not individual, that one will come back to when one has discussed the role of the elected representatives, what is to be included in the Constitution, and how we ensure that the political change of words is vital, because it is the understanding and the change of words that is the strength of an active democracy.

On several occasions, we have put forward proposals for a separate constitutional provision which establishes the inviolability of human dignity and the right to life and physical integrity of all people. The right to life and human dignity are a fundamental principle of our cultural heritage, and as a natural consequence, it should also have been enshrined in our constitution – not least this should have been natural at a time when we experience that human dignity and human life are more vulnerable than ever. For approx. A thousand years ago, Christian law was introduced in Norway. It paved the way for a new view of humanity, and with that it happened is marked the upgrading of human dignity. Over time, this view of man was further developed – we might say quality assured and updated – in response to new challenges. A process is still in progress. This understanding of what a human life is, suggests that everyone has the same inviolable value, regardless of integrity, gender, faith, age, civil status and ethnic origin. These values are also key elements of the 1948 Universal Declaration of Human Rights, to which the rapporteur also refers, and other international conventions. So far – in 2013 – there has not been a mood here in Stortinget to fall into the Norwegian Constitution, but the next big deal is invited to do by the fact that there are now proposals, not only the proposals of the Christian People's Party, but also other proposals, which address some of the same theme. The Christian People's Party and the undersigned do not doubt that the right to life and physical integrity has great upswing in the Norwegian people and also here in this Chamber. If we asked, we would probably confirm that everyone considers the right to security of life and body as fundamental. No one will protest against such a thought, where rather the danger is that we take these values as a matter of course, but in this area we know that nothing is of course. If we look around, we have to admit that human life is more threatened than ever. The use of violence is increasing both in our society and in other societies. Ethnic conflicts, human trafficking and slavery are no part of history, they are part of reality today. We are all concerned that ideologies that weaken respect for life and human dignity should get stuck, especially among younger people, and in such a situation there should be no reason to place less emphasis on respect for life and human dignity than before – rather on the contrary. However, the Christian People's Party believes that more than ever we need a tabular paragraph in our most fundamental law that promises the right to life highly, not because such a paragraph alone may lead to so much concrete in the short term, but because it will forever be an important reminder signal for everyone – and not least the legislators – of the fundamental values on which our society and our law will build. Our society is becoming increasingly complicated, not least in the field of technology. Researchers are still showing their innovation, and this is also true of medical research. Some of this research takes place in the border areas between life and death. The Biotechnology Act is still challenging us. Still, it is important to find the right balance between ethics and technology. In the debate on early ultrasound, we are also challenged to take the big debate on the sorting society. Biotechnology gives us opportunities and can do a lot for people. We also need to seize these opportunities, and we need to seize them while taking into account that biotechnology also touches on values that are central to our society, human value and a society with room for all. The right to life is the most fundamental of all human rights, and that's why I'm spending time talking about it today. Respect for human life and human dignity is also fundamental to our own cultural heritage, and it is the very bearer of our values. Our culture is built on the fact that all people have the same value, regardless of integrity, age, gender, civil status and ethnic origin. This right has shaped our legislation and ethical standards in our country. Man is entitled to both human dignity and life because it is a human being. That's the starting point for our attitude to the trend we're looking at today's beginning sorter society. Medical developments have made it possible to still face new problems, both in the first phase of life and at the end of life. An increasing number of diseases can be identified already at the stage of birth, and with an increasing number of advanced foetal diagnostics it is opened to sort out children on the basis of gender or disability. There are also many difficult ethical dilemmas around the end of life, for example. the duration of a treatment. Without a law that specifically protects life, there may be a risk that qualitative assessments of human dignity will be made. It's a development I want to warn against. I finish where I started, saying that for the Christian People's Party, the struggle for the fundamental law of the right to life is a core question. So I hope there are politicians also in the coming period who will raise the tab of the most fundamental of all values, namely the right to life and human dignity. Nevertheless, it is important that future actors in political life are also confronted with these issues and have to address them.

In Norwegian legal tradition, the individual has always been very strong. The rights of the individual have been very much stronger than in many other societies around us – from the Viking Age up to now – with farming, landscaping and larger and larger units, where one meets and discusses and finds agreement. But the point here is how important this is: point 1, in order for you to be equal in the democratic situation, but, point 2, you have to be able to exercise your opportunities as an equal person in this society, you actually depend on having a place to live. Housing is a material thing. It's not necessarily that the individual needs to own this dwelling, but it's actually of the things that we can politically also realize. For homes are physical things. They can be built, and we can count them. It's a little worse with both climate, environment, work and all these things that we can't necessarily adopt politically. But this is actually one of the absolutely fundamental things that we could have secured through political decisions, and that could have realized that everyone had a much better starting point to be equal also in terms of human rights in this country.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e616	Toll collection should be targeted at the lowest possible cost. ‘The investigation shows that this has not been realised.’ This was claimed by National Auditor Jørgen Kosmo when this investigation was presented to Stortinget on 11 December last year. The study also found that there has been a significant increase in the use of tolls over the past 10 years. While the drivers paid 3.2 billion. In 2002, the amount had increased to SEK 6.6 billion. kr in 2011, i.e. more than one doubling. The study, which aimed to assess the effectiveness of toll management during this period, concluded that toll collection does not take place at the lowest possible cost, and that as much as 12.5 pst. tolls contribute to operating costs. However, increased automation of toll collection has contributed to the achievement of the objective of least possible roadside disadvantages for road users. Automation was also intended to contribute to a reduction in operating costs. Unfortunately, that has not happened. The costs were reduced from 2002 to 2005, while they increased after 2005 and were above the 2005 level in 2011. A unanimous committee expresses its concern that the objective of toll collection at the lowest possible cost has not been achieved. We also note that in letters to the National Audit Office, the Council of State agrees with the conclusion that there is a potential for efficiency on the cost side. The Ministry of Transport points out that the proportion of road users using tiles is the factor that has the greatest impact on efficiency. In this context, it is pointed out that plans are being made to introduce a mandatory toll bar system for certain categories of vehicles with a permissible total mass exceeding 3,5 tonnes, i.e. mainly commercial vehicles. We heard a few minutes ago that the proposition was handed over to Stortinget, and it's also mentioned in today's VG. This could be a useful measure, which could also result in a significant reduction in the number of non-resident commercial vehicles that are currently not paying if we introduce a mandatory piece. Companies also have large losses on receivables as a result of the fact that road users do not settle separately. This amounted to as much as 172 million in 2011. kr. The National Audit Office also points out that weaknesses in technical solutions to the collection equipment have resulted in loss of income and that weaknesses in the performance of maintenance and operational services have resulted in lower performance. One company lost as much as 10.5 million. DKr due. faults in the collection equipment during the period 2008 to 2012. In addition, the National Audit Office criticises weaknesses in the direction and follow-up of the Ministry of Transport, where it appears that the Ministry gives little guidance signals about the toll management itself. I am pleased that a unanimous committee agrees with this criticism. The Ministry of Transport states to the Riksrevision that today the Directorate of Roads has the overall management responsibility as the toll management is organised, while the department only has an overall role. The Ministry claims that they can take a clearer responsibility for governance, and a unanimous committee requires this to be done. In addition, the report has revealed significant shortcomings in the competitive outsourcing of the acquisition of the operator by the companies. The Ministry of Transport claims that the Directorate of Roads should ensure better procedures for reporting on acquisitions and how this is followed up in the State Road Service. Regardless of the political position and regardless of what we may think about the use of tolls, we all agree that we need to have the most cost-effective operation of the collection, so that the money is spent as much as possible on what they should: building roads and other infrastructure. I therefore assume that the Ministry of Transport is now taking seriously the signal given both from the Riksrevisionen and from Stortinget, so that in the future we will have a much more cost-effective collection than we have today. The Progress Party believes that we need to finance mainly the development of national and county roads and other infrastructure using state money and without the use of tolls. We record that significant amounts of car and fuel charges are currently being levied, while only a small part of this is being returned to the road sector. At the same time, a growing number of tolling stations are constantly shifting new burdens on the drivers. The Progress Party disagrees with this development. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e810	This matter is a little special in the sense that a case has been raised in the committee and then carried out a hearing, with strong emphasis on this in the committee. The representatives of the government parties have provided an attitude which also contains polemics against the majority of the committee in order to raise a case and to organise a hearing. I want to say it on behalf of what is a majority committee, but that in the Storting is the opposition parties. I would also like to say in the introduction that this is not a hearing on so-called party nominations, as it was produced in some media. I do not intend to examine the party background by individual appointment, and that is not the issue here. That's not what we've been criticizing either. It concerns the professionalism of the Ministry of Economic Affairs in the way it manages appointments, in particular in companies where the State is not the sole owner but the partner with several, and where the companies are listed on the stock exchange. It is not about state ownership either. It has been said many times in the debate that this is an attempt to undermine state ownership etc. No, it's not. We're going to take that debate somewhere else. My party has made a number of proposals on, for example: full sale of Entra Eiendom, full sale of the Flytrain, full sale of Mesta and sale of SAS at an appropriate time – but then you might want to discuss whether there are buyers than how topical it is to privatize it. These are the positions I stand for, and that are shared by some parties – not all – in the opposition, but that is not what this matter is about. It's a siding. That matter is concerned, is the importance of professional and properly implemented state ownership. We start from three specific companies: Telenor, Kongsberg Gruppe and Entra. This does not imply criticising all administration. A number of other companies have been brought forward where the Chairman of the Board has been called upon to take the initiative of the Workers' Party, for example: a director of Mesta, who says he has nothing to criticize his ownership of, and that's okay. I'm happy about that. It was supposed to be missing, too. In fact, the task of controlling the size is to check where it does not work. We have pointed out here a number of errors that result in a proposal. I'll come back to that. So, in other words, in the subsequent debate I don't want to feel that we are criticising all of the state's ways of managing ownership, but we are drawing up some concrete examples. You could call it a random check. There is something any government council must find in it without being offended for that reason, or without the government parties taking too strong a word. The State currently manages funds through its ownership of more than 600 billion. kr, with thousands of jobs. The meaning is to have such a professional ownership that this does not reduce the value of the company, nor the value of the country's ownership of the companies. There is a risk that such a deterioration may arise as a result of State uncertainty — state ownership. There may be confusion about the role of the State, the protection of minority shareholders may be impaired, there may be recruitments or appointments that are not fully merit-based. There may be a lack of government willingness to change, and it may weaken a company that is in a tough process and in a tough market. Listed companies may – I stress – have lower values if the state is on the owner’s side. This is often referred to as the so-called government discount. This risk has been pointed out by the stock exchange manager on several occasions, and there has also been a discussion as to whether the exercise of ownership is precisely leading to such a decrease in value. This is the background for the hearing. The hearing was based on three specific issues, as I mentioned, and I allow myself to refer only to the views of the majority of the committee. I have some problems getting involved in the minority's view, even though I am the case rapporteur, but I am absolutely convinced that the minority will speak in a clear way. The majority of the Committee, i.e. the opposition parties in Stortinget, believe that the Telenor case is a clear example of discrimination against shareholders. We refer to the appointment process of the board of directors of the company – and this is therefore an international, listed, large company according to Norwegian and European circumstances – where the electoral committee, or the nomination committee, as one calls it, held 23 meetings. The nomination committee had reached seven competent candidates who had been interviewed after a thorough process. International consultants were also inside the picture. Following the completion of this process by the nomination committee, the Ministry required two candidates to be admitted to Telenor’s governing bodies. One candidate, the first proposed, was not accepted as qualified by the company. Then came the department with candidate no. 2nd@item: inlistbox He was accepted. Let me say it like that, to quote Einar Førde: Makta Rår – also in the shareholder community, and it is clear that when the majority winner makes proposal No. 2 and say here, but no longer, I think enough that professional people in the company understand what that means. A letter has been sent to the store, or at least the committee has been made aware of a letter from the last-mentioned Hallvard Bakke, who believes that he has been subject to the highest level of quality control, having been interviewed once by the nomination committee, and the interview lasted for an hour and a half. Let me say it like this: it's not the same selection process that the rest of the board has been through. That is what I mean by the fact that appointments are not merit-based, and that can create uncertainty in the company. This member of the Management Board has therefore been appointed without the same prior process as the rest of the Management Board. The members of the Committee of the Progressive Party and the Right say that this is clearly a discrimination which has led the ministry to break the premise in the bigges of ownership and to create uncertainty about the company. I think you're going to be looking for a couple more parallels. In the case of Entra Eiendom, we have gone into a slightly different issue. In short, there is the issue that went too long from the Council of State received a recommendation on instability in the interests that his relative, in order to use the expression of the law, was speaking as director of the company. This is an entire state-owned company, where contacts with the board of directors by extension and appointment are pretty close, and similar close by directorship, because the state owns everything. It took too long for him to assess his incapacitation until the Council of State was appointed. It's a little bit incomprehensible why it should take so long, because it's really a very, very simple process. It's not illegal to be incapacitated, it's not even critical. However, it is critical if you do not follow the advice and remove suspicions of incapacity by, for example. the appointment of a Member State Council by appointment or, as in this case, a director’s appointment, in which the State was certainly not directly involved, but it was, as the chairman also said, a close dialogue on the issue with the Ministry. Here are two quotes about ownership: ‘They’ — the State — ‘must be aware that they are part of a shareholder community and that it commits’, says the head of the Swedish Social Security Fund. And on the other hand, ‘But shareholders’ own involvement and respect for the processes are a prerequisite for transparency and efficiency in this work. Therefore, anchoring processes of the largest owners are not bad business management until the Electoral Committee has completed its work.» There is no doubt that the article and opinions of these members of the Norsk Institute of Directors support the criticism, what I would call the relatively mild criticism, which the majority of the Committee, the opposition parties, are addressing the Council of State in this context. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e1022	The Norwegian State has significant interests in Norwegian business: at least 600 billion. kr in companies in which the State is the sole owner or holds a holding. The government manages funds on behalf of the community and has influence over the management of large corporations with thousands of jobs. It is doubtful of great importance that the Storting is interested in and committed to how values are managed. For the Workers' Party, throughout our history, it has been a supporter that the state should have decisive influence over the Community's resources. The State shall be the owner – and an active owner – where necessary and useful. Equally important is that ownership should be controlled so that there can never be any doubt that everything is going right. Therefore, there would be no fundamental opposition from the government parties to opening up proceedings on the ownership exercise of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade as such if there were grounds for doing so. But it is not in this case. This is a case that was opened by the opposition to try to bring the council into disrepute. There is a deep conflict between the government parties on one side and the Right and the Progress Party on the other in the view of state ownership. The parties to the Government strongly disagree with the proposal of the Right and the Progressive Party to sell heavily in state ownership. On the contrary, we believe that state ownership should be maintained and exercised actively. This view has broad support in Norway, so the Right and the Progressive Party do not have popular support for their privatisation proposals. But that is precisely why it is more opportunistic – as we have just seen – for the Right and the Advance Party to attack the exercise of state ownership. If confidence in state ownership is weakened, it could facilitate a privatisation process. It is this game that has taken place in this case. I say, of course, it is absolutely legitimate for the Right and the Progressive Party to be in charge of such a privatisation policy, and we are fighting for it, but the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee in the Stortinget should not be the venue for such a political struggle. It is part of the usual political debate, of the various trade unions and of the European Parliament in plenary. The Trade Union Committees also have a customary responsibility to control the political handling of matters in the various departments. The fact that the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs travels and deals with a matter such as this one we now have undermines the division of roles that must exist between the Committee on Control and the Committee on Constitutional Affairs and the other committees of the European Parliament and its work. Therefore, the Government’s parties voted against the consideration of this matter, as it is presented in the Control and Constitutional Committee. How did it all start? On 9 October 2012, the Committee sent a letter to Minister for Economic Affairs and Trade Trond Giske on several issues related to Entra Endom, a state-owned full-time property company. The background was media information about the process of recruiting a new CEO of the company. I say that we were all shocked by the information that the CEO would have a salary of 5.3 million. kr, but we also know that a government council does not negotiate pay to the CEO of companies, nor in the state. But the cause of the opposition's anger was not this, of course. It was that the new CEO had a background in the Workers' Party and was Giske's friend. However, this fell to the ground when it turned out that Giske had declared himself inhabitable because Olsø was his friend, and that the Council of State did not think in any way about it. Despite questions and studies, it turns out that this was absolutely correct and concrete. In parallel with the Entra Eiendom case, attention was paid in the media to the process of electing board members also in two other companies in which the state is the largest owner: Telenor ASA, where the state's holding is 53.97 p.m. and Kongsberg Group ASA, where the State owns 50,001 pst. It is the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade that manages the state ownership of both these companies. According to NTB 23 October 2012 ‘Giske demanded last year to have his friend and party colleague Tore O. Sandvik placed on the board of Telenor, but without success’. According to the same NTB message, ‘have pressured the Kongsberg Group Technology Group to give Lo Leader Roar Flåthen space on the board. The board was not on the elections and there were no plans for changes. The case ended with Flöthen getting a newly created position as an alternate member. He thus gets to attend the meetings, but without having the right to vote. The fee is SEK 195 000 per year". In its reply to the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee, the Councils of State, among others, made: preparing the processes for the election of members of the governments of state-owned companies and clarifying that board members with broad social experience and political experience from different political parties have also been elected. Despite the well-informed answers, the majority of the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee decided at its meeting on 13 November 2012 to initiate preparations in a separate case for the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade. There were no indications that the Minister of Economic Affairs and Trade had gone beyond his powers and his responsibilities as a Council of State. Neither the Entra Eiendom, the Kongsberg Group, nor the Telenor statements were made in the media to the effect that the Council of State had managed the management of the company in breach of good offices or the established frameworks for the state's ownership policy. We can therefore see that the examination of the case and the consultation carried out have fully confirmed that this was a correct conclusion. In the hearings, there are no claims – I repeat: no claims – or information that substantiates the need for such a case in the committee. On the contrary, the hearings confirmed that the State Council had already answered written questions from the Committee. Government parties also opposed the invitation to the electoral committees of the state companies at the hearing. An election committee or a nomination committee of a listed company is one of the company’s organs enshrined in the company’s statutes. The work of an election committee is not part of the Council of State's duties for which he is responsible to the Stortinget. The internal work of the Electoral Committees is therefore not subject to the control of the National Audit Office. The work of an election or nomination committee is characterised by confidential cooperation with other representatives of the shareholder community. This is essential in order to carry out the work. Confidentiality is also necessary regarding the persons being evaluated and discussed in the Committee. A practice in which electoral committees are asked to explain their work in the Grand Duchy violates this confidentiality. If confidentiality practices change in the state-owned companies and thus total confidentiality cannot be guaranteed, it must be expected that many will see it as not desirable to be candidates for the board of directors. This would constitute a particular practice contrary to what is normal for professional corporate governance and could harm the recruitment of corporate governance. It could once again weaken the position of the companies and the value of the companies. Therefore, what really breaks the good governance of state ownership in this case is pushing the electoral committees to outsource their work in an open hearing in the control and constitution committee. A consequence of such a practice, if the State requires transparency in the dialogue between nomination committees and other committees and the State, may be that the State may no longer be able to participate in the committees. It will weaken the possibilities of continuing a serious and professional state ownership management, in line with what is being done today. The election of directors is one of the most important decisions in which an owner of a company is involved. It is therefore essential that the State be able to participate in the forms of work necessary for the proper functioning of nomination committees. It is not appropriate to ensure the state's interests or formally correct that the Storting enters into the election committee's internal discussions in companies in which the State has interests. The hearings showed that none – none – of the so-called electoral committees felt that the state council had broken the principles of good ownership. In addition to a point of view that we have heard a lot about, namely that the members of the board of directors have been late, the members of the electoral committee had nothing to say about the ownership exercise of the Council of State and of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade. At the same time, it was stressed that recordings were handled in a good and correct manner by the electoral committees. The current chairman of the nomination committee in Telenor, Anders Kärvestad, said in the hearing: ‘There is nothing in the guidelines or in the ownership notices on deadlines and on when these recordings are due, so there is no concrete guidance for that. The nomination committee in Telenor was of the opinion at the time it arrived late. It wasn't desirable. But we must also stress that we believe that we managed the situation correctly." Former Chairman of the nomination committee in Telenor, Jan Erik Korsjön, said: "What has come up here is that this recording of names came very late. But as we have said, we have to accept that. As it has also been said, I believe that we managed it in a good way." Member of the Election Committee of Kongsberg, Alexandra Morris, commented at the hearing on the state's input to the board member as follows: ‘This is not unusual for a large owner. It is common for a large owner to express opinions about members of the board. That was the time that was a little unfortunate." Morris also said: "We had several choices, as I said in the beginning. But we came up with a good solution for the company. Everyone knows the state has a majority. But I felt no pressure. I just felt that this was a situation, and here we had to find a good solution, which also safeguarded everyone's interests." The chairman of the election committee in Kongsberg, Sverre Valvik, said: ‘The whole background is, of course, that we got a proposal on the table that Roar Flöthen should be elected as a board member. We found it difficult to meet, but came up with a compromise. The background to this is that we wanted to take into account the wishes of all shareholders, including the state, in this context, and then we found that, in addition to the competences of the board from the past, the competence of the fleet would contribute positively." In other words, we can state that the solution of choosing Roar Flåthen as a permanent alternate member of the Governing Board in Kongsberg, with normal governing fees, was not a proposal from the Ministry of Industry and Trade, but a compromise proposed by the Election Committee. The chairman of the election committee at Kongsberg said in the hearing: ‘We did not follow the Ministry’s input, because it was choosing Flöthen as a member of the Management Board.’ On questions from the representative, Mr Becquerad, about the remuneration of this alternate, a decision was made by the election committee alone, or a response from the largest shareholder, Mr Valvik replied: ‘No, it was the Election Committee.’ Following the proceedings of the Committee and a full day’s hearing, no breach of the State’s guidelines on ownership management was detected. The only negative remark that remains is that on two occasions, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade has been recording candidates a little late. But neither is this a breach of the guidelines. In addition, it appeared that both electoral committees dealt with the proposals in a professional way and arrived at a unanimous position. It is also appropriate to mention in this context that the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Minister of Industry at the hearing had very good opportunities for the exercise of the country’s ownership management. Former president of the NHO and current director of Telenor, Svein Aaser, said: ‘When it comes to the relationship with the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade in Oslo, my view is that they exercise ownership in a very professional way, in line with the ownership environments I otherwise know.’ That's what Aaser said. Former Right-State Council Børge Burnde said: «I experience that the communication between the owner and the company has been after the book and correctly.» He also said, «When I then mention the Department, it also implies a characteristic of the Constitutional Council of State, and we have experienced a professional follow-up of Mesta as a group.» This is what happened at the hearing. This is what has been said by those who have been asked to come and speak to the Storting on the relationship between these companies, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and the Constitutional Council of State in the specific contexts that they were asked for. It is also true that in this case we have unfortunately experienced that representatives of the opposition parties in various contexts have promoted strong claims and accusations. I didn't get to that point in the line change – I hope we get the opportunity to get back to it during the debate. At Erna Solberg, Højre’s leader and parliamentary leader, Siv Jensen, Chairman and parliamentary leader of the Progress Party and Per Sandberg, Vice-Chair of the Progress Party and Head of the Justice Committee, the Right and Advance Party has used words such as ‘shaking’, ‘mafia states’ and ‘corruption’. What I am going to say, I am asking the Stortinget to bear in mind, namely: these claims were not tried to prove either through the examination of the committee or in the verification hearing. This was not asked at all – not one question, not one attempt to clarify whether it was really a basis for claiming from the leader of the largest opposition party on Stortinget that there were corruption-like conditions – not one thing in connection with this. There was no information either, of course, that supported this in any way. I must therefore simply note, unfortunately – and I am sorry to say now – that in this case the Right Party and the Advance Party have adopted a form of work in which serious accusations are first promoted in the media, with demands for treatment in the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee, but without following up these accusations in the formal control work. Such a working method further reinforces the impression that the Right Party and the Progress Party in this case are running a negative political campaign, and not, as the rapporteur tries to claim, a real control work. I believe that what I have said and covered in this post shows it very clearly. The Committee's opinion does not tolerate many things like this until it also affects the credibility of the Committee and the weights of the major and serious control matters of the Committee.

The starting point of the case is a series of media suggestions related to the ownership exercise of the Ministry of Industry and Trade. It has become something special, because the government parties have brought out the big defense cannons in an attempt to protect the Council of State Giske. I therefore think that there is a basis for saying a little about the framework of the activities of the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs, the background for opening up matters in our committee, and what makes this matter a little special. The starting point for this matter is that the Committee would examine whether the exercise of ownership by the State Council is within the premises of the Stortinge, inter alia. through the processing of the ownership message. The fact that the Storting can control the state's ownership exercise is questionable. They even process their own annual report from the National Audit Office on similar issues each year. The extent to which parliamentary control can go is initially up to the Committee to evaluate itself. However, it is clear that control extends at least as far as the constitutional responsibility of the Council of State. So in this case, there's no doubt that the ownership exercise in NHD is clear within that framework. Such ownership can hardly be controlled in any way other than what we have done here, by looking at practical examples of that ownership exercise. In the vote, the government parties wrote that there was no indication that the Council of State had ‘exceeded its powers’ at the time the committee opened proceedings. Martin Kolberg was also interested in it in his article instead. It's kind of a weird formulation. There were three things that were mentioned in the media, where government council Giske's ownership exercise was discussed. One was the allegation that Gishe had made sure that a alleged friend of himself would be placed on the board of directors of Telenor, after the election committee had finished its work. It was also claimed that he was not found qualified, after which the Council of State would have allegedly ensured the insertion of another person who was finally assessed as competent. The criticism was that the Council of State short-circuited the process of the company and called for changes to the Board of Directors in contravention of the initial suspension of the Electoral Committee, drawn up over several months, with thorough work and consideration of several dozen current candidates. It was also claimed that the Council of State had made sure that the LO leader was satisfied with a position in the board of the Kongsberg Group, without the responsibility of the board, but with compensation as a member of the board. It should have happened for a year when the board was not in elections. Furthermore, it was claimed that the Council of State could contribute to the fact that the Chairman, Vice-Chairman and a member of the board of Entra had been subjected to a coup with the aim of employing a alleged friend of the Council of State Giske as CEO of the company, without an ordinary procedure. The Council of State was incapacitated in all respects concerning him, but did not report this to the Governing Board of Entra until August. Only after the alleged coup trial was the Council of State appointed in Giske's place, despite the fact that Giske should have known about this conflict in the board since May. These three cases gave the impression of an unprofessional and random ownership exercise in large important companies where the State is a whole or a partner. It gave the impression of a large owner running over other owners and acting unprofessionally. State Council Geske rejected answering questions about this, as he would not deal with ‘anonymous claims’. In the view of the Progress Party, it would be a failure for the Control and Constitutional Committee not to consider whether these claims were true or not. It is in our opinion more than indications that something could be wrong. In the setting, the government parties also write: ‘It is very unusual for the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee to open proceedings on the basis of unconfirmed statements made in the media.’;» [[...]] It's directly wrong. Seven of the nine self-initiated control cases that we have dealt with in this Control and Constitutional Committee are the direct result of a possible unconfirmed look-up in the media. So it's the general rule, not the exception, that control matters are opened on the basis of media reviews. With some wonder I have registered that the Workers' Party believes that this control matter is really about politics, and not about control. From the side of the opposition parties, we have been quite unambiguous in dealing with this matter as a matter of control, the purpose of which is to find out whether the Storting's assumptions are met or not. It is sad that the Workers' Party is trying to create a political game about a matter that is so obviously a matter of control. Then we'd like to disagree on what kind of findings have been made and what conclusions to draw. But to operate that form of avoidance – and I want to use the term ‘mist’ – that the Workers’ Party has, in my opinion, operated in this case, does not serve the control function of Stortinget. It undermines the important function of the Committee. During the verification consultation, the media’s presentation of the three cases was largely confirmed. The course of events was frightening like what the media had referred to. In the Telenor case, the Council of State, Gishe, had asked the Election Committee to appoint a member of its own board of directors. In addition, the Council of State insisted on a new criterion for the selection and selection of members of the Governing Board. However, the Electoral Committee refused to suspend the proposal of the relevant State Council because he did not have the appropriate competence required by Telenor. Then the State Council asked the Committee to appoint another from its ranks. The election committee bowed down and did as the state council said. It is difficult to see such an instruction from the clearly largest owner as something other than a clear pressure. At the same time, the person who was hired avoided the thorough process that other elected board representatives had to undergo, including an interview with the chairman of the board, whom I was in place. It is no longer sufficient to be qualified to sit on such a board. It is crucial whether the competence and experience of the candidate meet a defined need in the work of the Governing Board and whether he is able to make a positive difference in the work of the Governing Board. So the election committee spent 8 months and 23 meetings finding an optimal composition for Telenor, and then being tested by the Council of State. I have seen that the Council of State has reacted both times when I mentioned that the person who was picked out was avoiding an interview with the director. The reason I point out this is that it became clear in the verification hearing that it was common practice in Telenor that those who were assessed as current board candidates also had a conversation with the board manager before they were cancelled. So it's one of the processes that he didn't carry out. It is unlikely that it is in the interests of the company to risk this kind of short-term process when selecting the company's senior management. The Council of State could make the recordings at the beginning of the process, with the Ministry represented in the Election Committee. In this context, I would like to refer to a very good chronicle in the Evening Post on 22 February, in which several central board representatives in different state companies give their views as to whether what I perceive is the importance of the State not overruning the electoral committees' processes, but rather contributing along the way in line with the assumptions. That's what the Telenor thing is about, not all this other pipe. In the case of the Kongsberg Group, the media production was also confirmed in the verification hearing. The Electoral Committee had no intention of replacing members of the Governing Board. There was an interval time, and no one had reported any desire to leave. The Council of State was represented in the Election Committee with representatives from the Ministry. Then it was demanded by the Council of State Giske that LO leader Roar Flöthen be appointed as a member of the board of directors of the company. The Electoral Committee opposed that. In a kind of dialogue, a compromise was reached in which Flöthen was appointed as an alternate, but with full governing compensation. This is a structure which neither the members of the Electoral Committee nor the State Council know whether it is used in other companies. As a general rule, directors are also paid for the risks they have as members of the Management Board, as well as for the work they do. An alternate does not have such a board of directors. The construction must therefore be considered to be very unnatural. The head of the election committee in the Kongsberg Group was clear that the solution that was chosen was not favourable, but that the company could live with it. In the Telenor and Kongsberg Group cases, the State that owns has overrun other owners, short-term internal processes and called for changes that the Company's Election Committee initially opposed. It builds up under the image of a non-professional ownership exercise. Director Bente Landsnes said to TV 2 that the government's ownership exercise could damage the value of Norwegian companies in the wake of these revelations. Trust in being a co-participant with the state of these major Norwegian companies is weakened, she told, after talking to several concerned foreign investors about what they perceived as unyielding Norwegian state behaviour. The Entra case differs from the other two things we've dealt with, because Entra is a whole-time state company, and the state then, of course, has sole responsibility for the appointment of the board. It usually happens, as I understand it, in dialogue between the board and the owner, but the owner of course has full authority over the selection of the board. The issue in the Entra case is also different. There is the question, as the Progress Party sees, whether the Council of State Gish acted neatly as the owner when the board was to appoint a new Executive Director, where one of the obvious candidates for the position was a close friend of the Council of State. Geske joined inhabil in April 2012 after advice from the Law Department, which is good. He then failed to inform the company of his incapacity. First, in a letter dated 17 August, the company was made aware of the inconsistency of the Council of State in all matters related to the appointment of the Executive Director. Meanwhile, the Directors of Entra had dialogue with an intruder owner about the recruitment process on at least two occasions. Therefore, it is not right when Kolberg claims that the owner was not involved in that period. The owner was also familiar with the major problems of the board through briefing from former director Grace Reksten Skaugen and chairperson Siri Hatlen. The owner was aware that the conflict was also about the appointment of the new CEO. The Council of State Gish explained that there was no need to appoint the Council of State, because this was a decision to be taken by the Board of Directors. However, both the former and the current Chairperson believe that it is obvious that the owner should be involved before the appointment of the Executive Director, not to change the responsibility for the addition, but to ensure that a manager who does not wish to oppose it, and to ensure that the Management Board is aware of all relevant information prior to the addition. This means that Gishe must have known that it was relevant to dialogue between the board and the owner before the appointment of the new managing director, and the new state council should have been appointed at the time Gishe declared to be involved. The absence of the Council of State also deprived the board of Entra of the possibility of dialogue with the owner after 17 August on a very demanding process, in a board that was divided and in full conflict. It's not a professional ownership exercise. I would like to comment so briefly on the second Telenor case that is affected in the setting. The background is Telenor's decision to contribute to the sale of TV 2. The Storting has previously received a statement from the Council of State on this matter, and many probably remember the claims about SMSes from a postgame at Lorry. That matter was also dealt with by the Committee in the context of the examination and audit of the annual audit of the Riksrevision. In retrospect, however, the chairman of Telenor, Harald Norvik, was cast as chairman. He even said in the verification hearing on this matter that there was a ‘direct connection’ between his handling of the TV 2 case and his departure as director. I would also quote directly from some of Norvik's statements about this: ‘I myself have been under the untimely pressure of the Council of State, and it is well known to the Committee, for it has dealt with it in the past. It was a pressure to make a different decision from what the board had decided to make, a decision that the board itself is responsible for making according to the government's governance principles. It's untimely pressure. It's happened. It may happen again, and it is a matter that needs to be discussed in public because it violates the country's ownership principles.» In the opinion of the Progress Party, this is the way to exercise ownership on a problematic basis. As the largest owner, the state has almost all the power in the company. That power is to exercise with caution, and it does not serve the state ownership to weaken governments of large state-owned shared companies by saying that either you do as the council says, or you have fired. The responsibility of the Management Board is to safeguard the interests of the company; the responsibility of the State is to build up the values of the community. This form of ownership exercise gives the opposite as a result. I also want to see how the Workers' Party handles control cases where their own government councils are under pressure. I have registered that there is a significant discrimination on the part of the Workers' Party in matters where the workers' party councils are in the search for any infringement, such as in this case, and when the State councils of the Socialist Left Party or the Central Party have the same focus on themselves: In the case of illegal party support for the Centre Party, the Workers' Party was committed to obtaining facts and cleaning up. That was strong and unifying criticism from the Committee. In the control case of the grant schemes, it was then the incoming SV manager and government councillor, Audun Lysbakken, who almost said he was sacrificed and had to leave. In the control case of the stability of the Støre Minister of Foreign Affairs in the allocation of funds to a foundation initiated by a friend of the Minister of Foreign Affairs, the Workers' Party went completely into the defence position, believing that everything was in the best order, and that the opposition was driving political games. We see the same thing in this case. The pattern is not completely unambiguous, but nevertheless clear. It will be very good in this position and the debate here today. Fortunately, everyone with access to the Internet can read what was said in the hearing and assess the media review of this matter. This is a well-meaning advice that I suppose will not be followed. Since the Workers' Party has tried to get this case to deal with different views of state ownership in general, I would point out that the Progress Party is not against state ownership. We believe, however, that one must have a conscious relationship with what kind of company the state should own, and that the state acts as a clean and professional owner of the companies that are still to be owned. Constructing a political debate on state ownership in this matter is entirely on the sidelines and is being incorporated into the series of attempts to misrepresent the Workers' Party. Them about it! Politicians live by trust. It also makes big companies. As the owner, the state must help build and strengthen the trust in the companies we own in whole or in part, not the opposite. That is why it is necessary and important that the rapporteur address the proposal for a decision of an overall opposition.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e1598	The intervention of the government parties in the control committee has been sea critical all the time I've been there. What has emerged in this discussion is that the chairman of the committee has said that if there had been any greater humility on the part of the Council of State, an probably avoided the matter. So we're facing a case where the constitutionally responsible state council has not shown the necessary humility, from the opposition's point of view, and it has become a matter. What is at the heart of the question is, of course, the underlying thing in the matter, the view of the extent of the country's ownership and the view of the exercise of state ownership. With regard to the extent of state ownership, there is a disagreement between the classical right forces and the classical social-democratic or socialist interests – and then with a centre party that has a pragmatic view of state ownership, where we have always seen it as right within, for example, the Telecom page, within the defence industry and within the building used by the State. We are therefore very confident in a tradition where we see state ownership as a necessity to complete the private ownership, which we otherwise want to be scattered on as many hands as possible. So with regard to the exercise of state ownership, which is the underlying theme here today, there are two companies that have been most in the foreground, namely Telenor, where the state has 53.97 pst. ownership, and Kongsberg Gruppe – the former Kongsberg Armed Factory – where the State owns 50,001 pst. What has evolved over time, which is at the heart of the matter, is that there has been a political understanding, independent of governments – I would stress it – of the fact that the state is to make capital available to these state-owned companies, where the board is to use that capital according to common rules for private business principles, where the principles are gradually increasingly inspired by the greatest possible return in a short time. What is positive – and it is very important to me – that has come out of this case, is that we have had a discussion on how to exercise the state ownership of companies with which there are also private owners. From the side of the government parties it is clear in the setting that we ‘considers it essential that State ownership performs the specific tasks of ensuring and further development on behalf of the Community of all or part of State-owned companies. Thus, state full or partial leases involve far more than the highest possible share price as is the case for listed companies. State ownership is also a strategic ownership.’ That's what's essential here -- that we've been clearly explained through the setting that when the state is inside the company as a partner, it's in order to take care of the particular circumstances that the community needs to take care of. So it's a strategic ownership, while a sailor has to deal with the provisions of the Share Act. This is the same as when any other owner is inside a company: one has two focus all the time. With regard to the exercise of ownership and those in the board of directors, we have shown from the government parties that under the general stock law, the board is responsible for determining the remuneration conditions of the Executive Director. In our view, therefore, we believe that: «the State must have an active role in the selection of directors in order to ensure that the Governing Boards follow up the country’s overall financial remuneration guidelines for managers and moderate wage developments.» In addition, the Committee as a whole has agreed on the following wording: «In several wholly-owned state-owned companies/undertakings, the overall financial compensation of management has reached an excessive level» — an excessive level. So it shows that we've finally had a discussion about both the content of state ownership, the exercise of it and the remuneration according to what I'm going to say is a progress in relation to the past. It is not the first time that government parties have pointed out the need for more active state ownership in relation to their interests. Once one has got it done, I expect one to go from comments to action on these points. This, I believe, is in the best tradition of how the members of the government parties act on the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs, to ensure that what is the will of the Storting is followed up in the day-to-day actions.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e1705	State ownership commits. It's true whether it's a whole-time or a part-time company. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e1787	I'm not sure if it's the Storting's problem that the representative Aasland has not finished reading the ownership message, in which it says a lot right and sensible, and which there was broad support here in the hall. It is also described that companies with strong government ownership pay a discount on the stock market in the form of a lower pricing of the companies. This discount reflects the political risk that exists for the other shareholders by not necessarily operating according to purely business principles. The more you have a «record» about doing business and business, the less this discount will be. The more turbulence, the more party political unrest brought into governing bodies, the greater the discount will be, with the loss indirectly imposed on taxpayers. Large international companies – which fortunately we also have here in the country – collect shareholder deposits from all over the world. It is absolutely essential for foreign investment to believe that the host country is subject to the obligations of all those who wish to conduct their business in the form of a limited liability company. Unfortunately, in particular, companies in former Eastern Europe have had to live with under-priced company values – between 20 and 25 and 30 pst.
