The representative, Lundtägen, said earlier in the debate that ownership confers governing rights. It is possible that his experience is limited to the landowner's usual exclusive ownership, but this is not a jungle that can stand alone in the case of limited liability companies. The form of a limited liability company is a way of collectively owning and interacting with shareholders who are not even aware of their name. It is precisely that the form of the limited liability company has rules that do not give unconditional control over the interests of the minority. Non-discrimination of shareholder groups is required in relation to information and in relation to board representation. It's a set of rules. There is a defined division of responsibilities and roles between the organs of companies as long as they are in operation. This means that if a nomination committee is set up, it is also the one that promotes suspension. So it has to know all the input from the shareholders if it's going to be able to do a good job. Although the majority of the company is involved, the interests and decisions of the general meeting must be taken by general meetings. The autonomy and integrity of the boards of directors must be respected between general meetings. Without these rules, the State would not have obtained co-investors. Without these rules, the state-owned and state-owned joint companies would not have had the value they have. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e2073	With the hope that this debate may have a little lower temperature than the debate we have just debated, I want to take advantage of the opportunity to explain how we are going to take care of some of the most valuable things we have, namely the children of our society. It's about how we take care of the children who may be in one of their most vulnerable and vulnerable situations. It's about having a welfare society that's best for those who need it the most. When our closest ones get seriously ill, most of us will experience anxiety and anxiety. When the relative is a child, fear and insecurity can become overwhelming. Sometimes the roles are turned upside down, and it's the children who become parents of their parents. Children in minority families feel that they must be interpreters and contact persons against the Norwegian auxiliaries when their mother and father do not speak Norwegian. It is particularly difficult when parents or siblings are seriously ill, and there are many and serious conversations. Meeting children or teenagers who have had to pass on to their parents the fact that they have serious — perhaps fatal — diseases gives the impression. In such situations, it is often said that the children are the weakest among us, but some of these children that are related are among the strongest. They carry the adult world's concerns and responsibilities on their shoulders, and quite simply many of them become too strong. We know that this is coming back to adult life. We know that many of those who have experienced that parents have had drug problems, parents have had mental problems, but are also seriously ill, even experience problems in adulthood because they have had to take so much responsibility. It is important that we establish that children should be allowed to be children even when Mom and Dad get sick — and also when Mom and Dad are unable to speak Norwegian. It's our responsibility to make sure that we have a public system that actually works like that. It is our responsibility to ensure that these children do not have to carry so heavily, and it is our responsibility to set up the safety net to catch them. But the first thing we have to do is just to see these kids. It's been a challenge. For many years we have not had a policy for relatives, especially a policy for children as relatives. In order to meet these challenges, inter alia: The right of the alternative state budget proposed to increase the allocation of the child-responsible health care businesses in order to ensure better follow-up of children as relatives. The previous Children's Ombudsman once said that it is the simplest and most banal things that are not done to ensure children a good childhood in our society. For example, when parents are sick or out of play in another way, the children need us as a society to be the big family they need. We no longer live in the time when grandma or grandpa was on the other side of the tunnel. The distances are larger, the help is needed in another way. Many good professionals have seen and taken care of children as relatives long before it somehow became a political matter. It's actually like we have a lot of good, conscious employees, but we know that hospitals and public services are fragmented, poorly coordinated, and then these are also often children as relatives. We may have to take self-criticism because we have not seen these children well enough up to now and have not taken good enough care of them. But once we've acknowledged that failure, we also have to do things better. That's why we've been working on closing the gaps in our security network in the last few years. One of the main measures taken is the establishment of the Children’s Best of Competence Centre. It's actually impressive to see how they've taken the challenge of spreading knowledge about how we can care for children as relatives. They impart knowledge and good examples to those who meet children as relatives in their daily work. They provide many of the professional adults with a tool that allows them to take better care of their children. The evaluation of BarnsBest has shown that they do the job with great enthusiasm. They provide many important measures to help children actually get help. The evaluation says that so much has been done that you might have to limit yourself a little – that’s really a pretty good joke. The title of the evaluation of BarnsBest is a quote from the employees: ‘We’re not asking, we’re just doing’. There might have been an ideal in this room too, that we did more than we asked. It turns out when the experience with BarnsBest shows that there is little systematic research-based knowledge about children as a relative. We may have for a period considered children related to drugs and psychiatry, but in other areas we haven't been as good. Luckily, for example, The Cancer Association has taken an initiative in this respect and is now systematically working much better with children as a relative of a cancer situation. It is still the case that getting a cancer diagnosis is a diagnosis that strikes hard. So we've established better models here. Then there is a requirement for child-responsive personnel in the specialist health service, and that is a step in the right direction. Child-responsible is a system responsibility imposed on institutions and it is a management responsibility to ensure that those responsible have the necessary capacity, competence and time. We can boast that we are the only Nordic country that has legal arrangements with child-responsors. But there are some challenges. Halsa and Kufås – in the book: Children as a relative, from 2012 – have conducted a focus group interview with the seven child managers of one healthcare company. Here three of these point out that the roles are diffuse, undefined and undecided. There is training in healthcare companies, but there are no instructions, some guidelines, procedures and headlines on the role of child-responsors in relation to other treatment providers. They further point out that it is a problem that child care professionals have not been directly connected to patients and relatives. Most of the meetings between patients, relatives, caregivers and caregivers take place without the involvement of child caregivers. Time pressure and production requirements have an impact on child-focused work. So it can still be random and unsystematic. So it's a desire for larger systems and guidelines. The second theme highlighted in this interview is that of the minority children in particular, namely the lack of interpreters in our society — and even less the use of interpreters in our healthcare system. I know that the representative Lise Christoffersen at the national meeting in the Workers' Party in 2011 was given an impression of the ban on children being used as interpreters in the public sector. In 2012, the evening post came up to hear how it had gone. I quote from the notice: ‘Minors are used as interpreters of decision-makers, teachers, hubs and in cases where children are related to convicted persons.’ Although the Workers' Party proposed an interpretative ban, little has been done. At least I intend to give my support to the initiative of Lise Christoffersen and say that we need to do something more about this. It is about focusing on and having at its disposal adequate skills. We cannot continue to have a situation where children are used in the most difficult situations, and are the ones to communicate the heavy and difficult to their parents. I therefore hope that the Minister of Health will address both the question of how we strengthen the professional competence of health care businesses and ensure that we avoid putting children in such a difficult situation, and how we can ensure that children of minority parents are assured that they are the ones who need to convey the most difficult messages that parents get in our society.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e2123	I would first like to thank the representative, Mr Solberg, for raising an important theme that I believe involves many people in the Chamber. There are several pioneers sitting in this room who have helped to make this a central area of action. It is undoubtedly a great burden for the children when their parents become seriously ill. The answer is no. Children shall not be an interpreter for sick family members. As of 2013, I have ordered hospitals to ensure that no children under the age of 18 are used as interpreters in the specialist health service through the so-called enterprise protocol. This is also fixed in the Storing Message See me! An overall drug policy that we are going to debate later this evening. This is independent of the diagnosis of parents or other relatives. Exceptionally, they may be used as an interpreter in acute quotations if there is a risk to life and health. The Directorate for Health has developed a guide for managers and health care staff on communication via interpreters who do not use children as interpreters. A working group chaired by the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion states that children should be required to have specific protection against being used as interpreters in the public sector and to discuss the introduction of a ban. The report shall be followed up by the Minister responsible. So there's a good deal going on in the follow-up to this case. The discussion of health business resources is part of a budget debate. Today I want to inform the Storting about measures for children as a relative of the government. I also want to draw up some future perspectives. The health service's task is to treat patients. Many patients have minor children who are affected by their parents ’ disease. The child’s sense of security, satisfaction and adequate care is strongly linked to the family’s situation. The Soria Moria Declaration states that we should work towards the follow-up and help of children with mentally ill and addicted parents. We have worked widely to achieve that goal and have extended it to all children with ill parents. Children of mentally ill, addicted and seriously ill and injured parents are all vulnerable and vulnerable groups, and it was well described by the interpellant. New measures have been taken in line with Storting’s appropriations. These are more than doubles, from 15 million. SEK in 2007 to 31 million. kr in year. From 2012 to 2013, they increased by another 10 million. kr – a doubling compared to the Right’s alternative budget proposal of 5 million. kr, if there is to be a reference. The review to which the inter-pellant refers is based on a 2010 survey. In the same year, children were given a stronger legal status as a result of changes in the Health Personnel Act and the Special Health Service Act. Children should receive better follow-up when their parents receive health care, regardless of where they live. All health professionals are obliged to contribute to the needs of their children. Health professionals shall not assume responsibility from their parents. As far as possible, parents themselves should take care of children. The task of the healthcare professional is to determine whether the patient has children, whether or not someone takes care of the children, to assess whether the children need follow-up, to provide the patient with information and guidance and to assist the child in providing information or other follow-up if the parents so wish. Hospitals and other specialised health services shall have child-responsible personnel responsible for coordinating and promoting the work of the patient’s children — in other words, a significant increase in this area. I am happy to be able to inform the Storting about a comprehensive work in this field in all health regions. From being ‘invisible children’ before 2007, children who are related are placed on the agenda throughout the country. This has raised awareness and interest in the prevention of children who have sick parents. Follow-up of children gives positive ring-effects to parents when receiving treatment. The concerns are getting smaller, and they feel cared for. There shall be a large reward for hospitals which also include follow-up of siblings to seriously ill children. All public hospitals and other health institutions with agreements with hospitals, including drug services, have child-responsors. The work of setting up child-responsors at private rehabilitation institutions is under way. Then, of course, we have to make this system work, so that people with this responsibility take it seriously. Health professionals have a position that makes it possible to see the children early, and they have experience in helping. Already skilled and engaged health professionals have received a number of tools through networks of expertise, courses and seminars, circulars, e-learning programmes, information material for children, websites, treatment lines and literature. This does not mean that we cannot get even better, and therefore the work must be further developed. We need to ensure an even better dissemination of competences across healthcare. The interaction reform includes children as relatives. The cooperation agreements between municipalities and hospitals refer to procedures for the care of minors in connection with accommodation, stay and outsourcing. Our largest hospital, Oslo University Hospital, has established a council for children as relatives. The interaction perspective and joint responsibility for hospitals and municipalities to care for the children are laid down in that mandate. A good childhood will last for generations. It also does a bad childhood. Year-on-year adulthood plays a major role in the developmental capacity of children. We therefore need to catch children with addicted and mentally ill parents early and take action to prevent children from developing problems themselves. Children who have been subjected to violence, abuse, care failure or have addicted parents are highly likely to develop similar problems themselves. This is a tragic reality. We need to increase our knowledge of children, and we need to strengthen our knowledge of the prevention of drug abuse in children. In 2007, we set up two trials in the North Trøndelag hospital and the West Viken with lowthreshold measures to follow up pregnant women and mothers with drug dependence and mental problems. Children are followed up from birth to school in close cooperation with other services. Projects received positive evaluations and became permanent from 2013. These measures bridge the gap between service provision and serve as support for families. Users seem to be satisfied. Two similar measures will be established in the West and North of Health during the year. We want to put this kind of low-threshold action in place across the country in a few years. I am pleased that a unanimous health and care committee in the setting up of the drug notification supports the work of setting up several such measures. So I'd say we're getting far. However, we need new knowledge. Children who are related from 2011 are a strategic research area in the Norwegian Research Council. Four major research projects will provide new answers. We're going to get knowledge of the meeting between registrars and children as relatives. We will learn more about early intervention, participation and development of skills around children of seriously ill and addicted parents. We need to know how social networks can play a role in families where a parent is or has been affected by cancer. We need to know about the extent of children with seriously ill or addicted parents: who are they, what needs do they have, what help do they get, and what benefits do they get from helping them? Evidence shall be provided of the effect of the legislative changes that strengthen the legal position of children. Six hospitals with large geographical distribution are involved in this research. A nationwide survey of the living conditions of children and young people with drug-dependent parents should provide knowledge of children and young people’s experiences and experiences. How do these children master everyday with their addicted parents? What are the habits of the children? When do they make their own debut with drugs? How do they experience any contact with the auxiliaries? These are important questions we need answers to in order to put forward more targeted actions in the future. Three organisations, BAR — Children of drug users — Adults for Children and the National Children’s Protection Association, make representations and follow-up. The ambition is clear: we must prevent drug problems from being inherited. Finally, once a year I am delighted to meet the healthcare users through a separate contact forum. These meetings provide useful input into the creation of better services, and they underline the user perspective that we have focused on in several key documents. So it is that the UN Children's Convention states that children have the right to evolve, to be heard and last, but not least, to express their own desires. The announcement of good quality and safe services, submitted before Christmas, gave the course for a new user role. This role must also include children. Children with sick parents should feel cared for, seen and heard. They're experts on their own lives. Therefore, we need to listen more closely to their experiences in a form that is adapted to children. I have a strong desire to listen to them, and will therefore invite children to meet on this theme in the different parts of the country where we follow it up, so that we get experience with the services I am responsible for, and they have enjoyed.

In 2011, the Public Health Institute estimated that 260 000 children have parents with a mental disorder that could go beyond daily functioning. About 70,000 parents have alcohol abuse. In addition, figures from the Cancer Association show that 4,000 Norwegian children experience cancer in a close family every year. 800 children experience that one of the parents dies of cancer every year. It is positive that measures for children who are related are strengthened, but we know that there are still failures. Children must have a happy childhood, they are our future. Therefore, it is important that sufficient resources are put in place to help and support children who experience traumatic and sad events when mother or father no longer acts as parents and caregivers. In the past, the Children’s Ombudsman has, among other things: address the issue of the use of children as interpreters for their parents. In autumn 2010, the Children’s Ombudsman wrote a letter to the Ministry of Children, Gender Equality and Inclusion. Some facts and quotes from the letter come here: Children are used as interpreters for their parents in parts of the public sector, and this is very negative both for the child and for the case of their parents. Case handlers are not trained in the use of interpreters. Interpreters are too poorly qualified. Interpret access is unstable. The interpreter booking procedures are too poor. Persons in need of interpreters have unqualified persons to interpret. The Children’s Ombudsman wrote: ‘The Children’s Ombudsman wants the authorities to consider imposing a ban on the use of children as interpreters in the public sector. [[...]] This will prevent children from being placed in situations where they are given access to information they should not have had for reasons of age, parental/child relationship and the child’s need for protection.» The Ombudsman also had an expert meeting with young people who had their own experience of being used as an interpreter for parents or other relatives. All of them had been used as interpreters in matters of some sensitive nature or matters they had nothing to do with. Everyone pointed out how negative this would have been for them, and how incredibly important it is with good and qualified interpreters. This means that children should not and should not be used as interpreters for their parents – this is because they are not qualified and because they still have to be allowed to be children. There is too much stress and stress for a child to have to go through something like this. Using children as interpreters in health services can at worst put life and health at risk. One study indicates that every third doctor sometimes or often allows the patient’s children to interpret for their parents. The Cancer Association believes that although interpreters have been agreed during the meeting with their parents, the children may still have to assist their parents in meeting with the public. We see an increasing trend where more young people drop out of school. Common to many of these is that they have or have had difficult family situations to deal with – many of them just because they often have to be with their parents and take care tasks that are far outside and beyond what we, as a welfare society, expect of a child. These are children who have to wait for childhood, precisely because they often have to help their parents in addition to many of these children having to take on much greater responsibility in and for the family. It hurts to know that this is still happening, and that it does, is almost incomprehensible. Therefore, this theme needs to be raised and given a much stronger priority in the future. We simply don't have the money and time to wait any longer. These are children who deserve a much better start in their lives, where, as a society, we need to set up and ensure that there are enough interpreting services – including at the country's hospital. There is, but not enough. These are today's great heroes who have not yet been seen and taken seriously. Now, on behalf of all these children, I hope that action will be shown. Children deserve to be children and did not have to wait for their childhood. If this continues to happen, I hope that the pressure will be put on the use of interpreting children to stop immediately.

What we are now seeing is that health businesses are actually changing their content by reducing treatment time and reducing the social content of treatment. This is contrary to what the Stortinget thought in the context of Rusreform II, where the Stortinget was clear that the institutions should strengthen their health skills, but that it should not go beyond multidisciplinary social rehabilitation. But health businesses ignore it, and one reduces the treatment time and the content of the treatment, without the government seemingly wanting to do anything about it. One sees that we still have about two and a half thousand drug addicts in line for treatment. At the same time, good treatment institutions are being laid down. The right considers that a significant change is necessary for this patient group, and that change is about giving free treatment options, so that these patients can actually have the opportunity to choose the treatment institutions that they themselves believe in among those who are officially authorised and that they can use the capacity that is available. Today, if drug addicts choose a different treatment institution from the one they are offered, they move to the back of the queue and risk waiting this year. Everyone understands that by telling motivated addicts that you should come to the institution for which you are motivated, you have to wait this year, there is a great likelihood that the motivation is gone before the treatment offer exists, in reality. Therefore, one of the statistics also shows that there are no patient groups where there is so much disqualification from granting rights to actual attendance at treatment as in this patient group. Another main challenge, of course, is for these patients the transition from specialist healthcare to community services or through Nav. In terms of housing, activity provision, outpatient follow-up and further social follow-up. But for this group, the lack of quality assurance of the transition is life-threatening. We see that the majority of overdose deaths in Norway occur immediately after discharge from a treatment institution or prison. So today's practice is life-threatening for this patient group. This big message does not provide any concrete answers to the challenges, on the contrary. ParlaMint-NO_2013-03-18.ana.ud149e3057	I am very pleased that the proposal to expand the group of children and young people who are being exempted from property taxes from compensation for serious personal injury and loss of dependants is being agreed today. As the case-speaker said, it is one of the consensual committees that advises the case. The case should take effect from 2012, which is also very important. We discussed a similar Document 8 proposal in December 2012, and we decided to attach it to the protocol. It was simply because the department was already dealing with it, and there were hearing suggestions. The hearing proposal and the legislative proposal we are discussing today actually go a little further than what the Document 8 proposal did. In the legislative proposal, the limit has been removed completely, while it was reduced to 15 pst. in the proposal. Eleven have responded to the call, and everyone who has made comments supports the proposal. The case spokesperson mentioned it himself at the end of his post, but I just want to comment on the comment they have written when it concerns the Loan Box. Under current law, there are exceptions for the need to test scholarships for higher education students, which means that the grant will not be reduced by replacement and insurance payments as a result of personal injury to the applicant himself or to the spouse. That derogation shall also cover upliftment paid under the Law on Victim Compensation of Victims or the Law on Compensation of Damage. Similarly, grants to students in further training will not be reduced as a result of receiving such compensation and insurance payments. Actually, I just want to end by saying that I am also very happy that this matter is decided in such a short time, and that it will take effect from 2012, and I would like to thank the case spokesman for a good job.
